I’ll see the bill, or I’ll think about paying it, and then I’ll put it off like, “I’ll do that after dinner,” or “I’ll do it when I sit down,” and then next thing I know there’s a late notice and I’m mad at myself because it wasn’t like I didn’t have any warning.\n\nAnd with my kids, that one bothers me more emotionally. I’ll tell them I’ll pick something up or bring something home, and I mean it when I say it. Then I come home and they ask about it, and I realize I completely forgot. It makes me feel awful, because to them it probably feels like I didn’t care enough to remember, but in my head it just slipped away.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory lapses and mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s been a real problem since I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago, something like that.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I forgot things before then like anybody does, but that’s when it started feeling like, “Okay, this is actually causing problems.” Once I had more reports, more procedures to keep track of, more things where if I missed a detail it showed up later, it became much more obvious. So I’d say around 14 months ago is when it really became noticeable and started affecting me in a bigger way.\n\nDoctor: Have these mistakes and memory lapses affected your relationships with your family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I don’t think it’s destroyed anything, but it definitely causes problems.\n\nAt home, the biggest thing is people feeling like they can’t count on me to follow through. Like if I say I’m going to do something, there’s this look sometimes, or a comment like, “Are you actually going to remember?” And I get why they say it, but it still stings. Because I’m not trying to blow people off or make extra work for anyone. I’ll genuinely mean it when I say I’m going to handle something, and then if I forget, it turns into an argument or this whole thing where I’m defending myself, but I also know I messed up.\n\nIt adds tension, especially with family. It makes me feel like I’m letting them down in these small repeated ways, and then those small things build up. People start reminding me more, or checking behind me, and then I get irritated because it feels like I’m being treated like a kid, but honestly I can’t fully blame them because I haven’t been reliable with some of it.\n\nAnd work-wise, it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review. So it’s not just me being hard on myself or thinking, “Oh, I made a couple mistakes.” It’s documented now, which was embarrassing and kind of scary. It made me realize this is affecting how people see me, not just how I feel inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling tense or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get tense, definitely, but I don’t know that I’ve paid close attention to whether my heart is racing or if I’m sweating or trembling.\n\nLike, if I’m stressed, I mostly notice it as being keyed up in my head, if that makes sense, or feeling wound up. But the physical stuff you’re asking about… I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a clear pattern. Maybe it’s happened and I just didn’t clock it that way, but I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these mistakes and memory lapses are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it’s interfering pretty significantly, especially at work. Work is where it feels most serious, because there are real consequences, but personally it’s wearing on me too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it that way, exactly. The point where it became a real problem was when I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago now, roughly.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I’m sure I had mistakes here and there like anybody, but it didn’t feel like this same ongoing issue. Since taking on that role, it’s been pretty consistent. There might be a better week or a day where I feel more on top of things, but I can’t think of a period where it just went away and I was like, “Okay, that’s over.” It’s more like it keeps showing up, and the setting I’m in now makes it harder to hide or recover from.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes and memory lapses are primarily caused by worrying about making mistakes or fear of not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry after I’ve made a mistake, or if I realize there’s something I might have missed, but I don’t know that the worry is what causes it in the first place.\n\nIt feels more like… I’ll be trying to keep track of several things, and then something slips, and then the worry kicks in afterward because now I’m thinking, “Did I forget something else?” or “Is this going to come back on me?” But I wouldn’t confidently say the mistakes happen because I’m afraid of messing up.\n\nThere are definitely times where I’m trying hard to do something correctly and I still miss a detail, which is frustrating. So I guess I don’t know. The fear might make me more tense once I notice a problem, but I haven’t really noticed that being the main reason the lapses happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself disorganized, having trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines, beyond just forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that from the forgetting, honestly. Like, when you say disorganized, do you mean my space being messy, or more like not having a good system for what I’m supposed to do next?\n\nBecause I wouldn’t say I’m completely chaotic all the time, but I do feel like keeping track of things can get kind of… scattered. Sometimes I’ll have things in different places, like notes or reminders, and then I’m not sure which one I’m supposed to trust or what I already followed up on. But I don’t know if that’s disorganization exactly or just me trying to compensate and not always doing it well.\n\nAppointments and deadlines, I usually try to put things somewhere so I don’t lose track of them, but if there are a lot of moving pieces, it can feel like I’m holding it all in my head and then it gets messy. A random thought about something at home will pop in, like something I need to check or something I forgot to mention, and then I’ve drifted off the task without really deciding to.\n\nThat’s the frustrating part, because it’s not always that I’m bored or avoiding it. I might actually be interested in what I’m reviewing, or know it’s important, but my attention just kind of slides away. Then I have to reread the same note or go back over the same numbers because I’m not sure where I left off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to complete tasks because you lose interest or get distracted, rather than because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s closer to what happens. It’s not usually like I’m sitting there frozen because I’m worried I’ll do it wrong. It’s more that I start doing what I’m supposed to do, and then something else grabs my attention or feels urgent, and the original thing just kind of drops off.\n\nLike if my manager gives me three or four things to handle, I can start out really solid. I’ll be like, okay, I need to talk to this rep, send that follow-up email, update the coaching note, and check on whatever number or account issue. And I’ll usually begin with one of them right away. But then somebody walks up with a question, or there’s a call issue, or I notice something else that seems like it needs to be handled, and I get pulled into that.\n\nThen later—sometimes much later—I’ll realize I never sent the follow-up email, or I didn’t close out the coaching note, even though in my head it felt like I was “on it.” That’s the part that bothers me, because I didn’t mean to leave it unfinished. It’s not that I stopped because I didn’t care or because I was scared of making a mistake. It’s more like the task lost its place in line once something else interrupted me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes and forgetfulness would happen less often if you didn't worry about the potential consequences of making an error?\n\nPatient: I don’t think the worry is the main driver, honestly. I mean, I do worry after the fact, because there are consequences if I mess up a sales report or miss something I was supposed to check. But the mistake itself usually happens even when I’m trying to be careful.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep making these dumb mistakes that feel like they shouldn’t be happening. Like I’ll copy numbers from the team dashboard into a sales report, and later I’ll realize I grabbed the wrong number or put it in the wrong spot. Or there’ll be a procedure I’m supposed to review, and I’ll think I went through it, but I missed a line that mattered.\n\nAnd that’s what frustrates me, because in the moment I’m not thinking, “Oh no, what if I make an error?” and then messing up because I’m anxious. It’s more like I genuinely believe I checked it, or I thought I was paying attention, and then somehow something obvious slipped past me. So if I didn’t worry about consequences, I don’t know that it would happen less. I might feel less stressed about it, but I think the copying the wrong numbers or missing the line would still happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're supposed to be sitting still or focused on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice a lot, especially in meetings or anytime I’m supposed to just sit there and pay attention for a while.\n\nLike if we’re in a longer meeting at work, even if the topic matters and I know I need to listen, after a while it feels like my body is buzzing on the inside. I don’t know if that sounds weird, but it’s like I’m sitting still on the outside only because I’m forcing myself to. I’ll start shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my foot, messing with a pen, adjusting how I’m sitting over and over. Sometimes I’ll catch myself doing it and try to stop, but then I feel even more uncomfortable.\n\nAnd if the meeting goes on too long, I’ll start looking for a reason to get up, like grabbing water, going to the restroom, checking something at the printer—anything that makes it seem normal to move around for a minute. It’s not even always that I want to leave the meeting. It’s more like I need to reset my body because sitting still starts to feel almost physically irritating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not like I’m trying to be rude, but it happens more than I’d like, especially in meetings.\n\nSomeone will be explaining something, and I’ll think I already know where they’re going with it, so I jump in with an answer or a response before they’ve actually finished. In my head it feels like I’m being helpful or keeping things moving, but then they’ll finish their thought and I realize I answered the wrong point, or I misunderstood what they were actually asking. And then I’m sitting there like, okay, I just cut them off and also didn’t even answer the right thing.\n\nIt happens with my team too. Like if a rep starts telling me about an issue, I’ll sometimes jump to the solution before they’ve gotten through all the details. Then they’ll say, “No, that’s not the part I mean,” and I have to back up and let them finish. I do try to catch myself, but a lot of times the response is already coming out before I’ve really thought it through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. That’s actually one of the things I notice a lot, especially in meetings or anytime I’m supposed to just sit there and pay attention for a while.\n\nLike if we’re in a longer meeting at work, even if the topic matters and I know I need to listen, after a while it feels like my body is buzzing on the inside. I don’t know if that sounds weird, but it’s like I’m sitting still on the outside only because I’m forcing myself to. I’ll start shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my foot, messing with a pen, adjusting how I’m sitting over and over. Sometimes I’ll catch myself doing it and try to stop, but then I feel even more uncomfortable.\n\nAnd if the meeting goes on too long, I’ll start looking for a reason to get up, like grabbing water, going to the restroom, checking something at the printer—anything that makes it seem normal to move around for a minute. It’s not even always that I want to leave the meeting. It’s more like I need to reset my body because sitting still starts to feel almost physically irritating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not like I’m trying to be rude, but it happens more than I’d like, especially in meetings.\n\nSomeone will be explaining something, and I’ll think I already know where they’re going with it, so I jump in with an answer or a response before they’ve actually finished. In my head it feels like I’m being helpful or keeping things moving, but then they’ll finish their thought and I realize I answered the wrong point, or I misunderstood what they were actually asking. And then I’m sitting there like, okay, I just cut them off and also didn’t even answer the right thing.\n\nIt happens with my team too. Like if a rep starts telling me about an issue, I’ll sometimes jump to the solution before they’ve gotten through all the details. Then they’ll say, “No, that’s not the part I mean,” and I have to back up and let them finish. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so—at least in the way I talk in meetings. I don’t know if I’d describe it like I’m out doing reckless things all the time, but I definitely act before I’ve fully thought it through when someone else is talking.

Like someone will be halfway through explaining something, and I’ll already have this answer forming in my head. I think I know where they’re going, so I just jump in. In the moment it feels almost automatic, like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose the thought or the conversation is moving too slowly. But then two seconds later I realize they weren’t even asking what I thought they were asking, or they had another detail coming that changes the whole point.

And then I feel embarrassed because I’ve cut them off, and sometimes I’ve answered the wrong thing completely. It’s frustrating because I don’t mean to be disrespectful. I’m not trying to talk over people. It’s more like my mouth gets ahead of the part of me that should wait and listen to the rest of the sentence.

I don’t think it’s destroyed anything, but it definitely causes problems.\n\nAt home, the biggest thing is people feeling like they can’t count on me to follow through. Like if I say I’m going to do something, there’s this look sometimes, or a comment like, “Are you actually going to remember?” And I get why they say it, but it still stings. Because I’m not trying to blow people off or make extra work for anyone. It’s not just work stuff. I’ll see the missed call or the voicemail and think, “Okay, I need to call them back,” and then somehow the day keeps going and it just disappears from my head until later, or until there’s another message.\n\nBills are another one. I don’t mean I don’t know they exist. I’ll see the bill, or I’ll think about paying it, and then I’ll put it off like, “I’ll do that after dinner,” or “I’ll do it when I sit down,” and then next thing I know there’s a late notice and I’m mad at myself because it wasn’t like I didn’t have any warning.\n\nAnd with my kids, that one bothers me more emotionally. I’ll tell them I’ll pick something up or bring something home, and I mean it when I say it. Then I come home and they ask about it, and I realize I completely forgot. It makes me feel awful, because to them it probably feels like I didn’t care enough to remember, but in my head it just slipped away.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory lapses and mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s been a real problem since I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago, something like that.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I forgot things before then like anybody does, but that’s when it started feeling like, “Okay, this is actually causing problems.” Once I had more reports, more procedures to keep track of, more things where if I missed a detail it showed up later, it became much more obvious. So I’d say around 14 months ago is when it really became noticeable and started affecting me in a bigger way.\n\nDoctor: Have these mistakes and memory lapses affected your relationships with your family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I don’t think it’s destroyed anything, but it definitely causes problems.\n\nAt home, the biggest thing is people feeling like they can’t count on me to follow through. Like if I say I’m going to do something, there’s this look sometimes, or a comment like, “Are you actually going to remember?” And I get why they say it, but it still stings. Because I’m not trying to blow people off or make extra work for anyone. I’ll genuinely mean it when I say I’m going to handle something, and then if I forget, it turns into an argument or this whole thing where I’m defending myself, but I also know I messed up.\n\nIt adds tension, especially with family. It makes me feel like I’m letting them down in these small repeated ways, and then those small things build up. People start reminding me more, or checking behind me, and then I get irritated because it feels like I’m being treated like a kid, but honestly I can’t fully blame them because I haven’t been reliable with some of it.\n\nAnd work-wise, it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review. So it’s not just me being hard on myself or thinking, “Oh, I made a couple mistakes.” It’s documented now, which was embarrassing and kind of scary. It made me realize this is affecting how people see me, not just how I feel inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling tense or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get tense, definitely, but I don’t know that I’ve paid close attention to whether my heart is racing or if I’m sweating or trembling.\n\nLike, if I’m stressed, I mostly notice it as being keyed up in my head, if that makes sense, or feeling wound up. But the physical stuff you’re asking about… I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a clear pattern. Maybe it’s happened and I just didn’t clock it that way, but I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these mistakes and memory lapses are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it’s interfering pretty significantly, especially at work. It’s not just little embarrassing moments anymore where I forget something and then fix it later. It’s gotten to the point that my manager actually put it in a formal review, which was… honestly, that hit me pretty hard. Once it’s written down like that, it feels less like “I’m having a rough stretch” and more like “this is affecting my job.”\n\nAnd at home, it’s not like I can’t enjoy anything ever, but it definitely takes some of the ease out of things. There’s conflict because people feel like they can’t count on me to follow through. I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then if I forget, it turns into frustration or reminders or people checking up on me. Then I feel bad, or I get defensive, and it kind of ruins the mood.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s affecting both. Work is where it feels most serious, because there are real consequences, but personally it’s wearing on me too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I worry about things, but usually there’s some reason for it, at least in my mind. Like if something is actually pending or I know there’s a chance I forgot something, then yeah, I might keep thinking about it. But just worrying out of nowhere, for no clear reason… I’m not sure.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts as “no apparent reason,” because sometimes the reason is kind of vague, like just a sense that I’m missing something or that something’s going to come back on me. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about everything in general all the time. It’s more tied to specific situations, from what I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where these mistakes and memory lapses stopped happening for a while, or have they been consistent since they started?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say they’ve really stopped for any meaningful stretch, no. The point where it became a real problem was when I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago now, roughly.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I’m sure I had mistakes here and there like anybody, but it didn’t feel like this same ongoing issue. Since taking on that role, it’s been pretty consistent. There might be a better week or a day where I feel more on top of things, but I can’t think of a period where it just went away and I was like, “Okay, that’s over.” It’s more like it keeps showing up, and the setting I’m in now makes it harder to hide or recover from.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes and memory lapses are primarily caused by worrying about making mistakes or fear of not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry after I’ve made a mistake, or if I realize there’s something I might have missed, but I don’t know that the worry is what causes it in the first place.\n\nIt feels more like… I’ll be trying to keep track of several things, and then something slips, and then the worry kicks in afterward because now I’m thinking, “Did I forget something else?” or “Is this going to come back on me?” But I wouldn’t confidently say the mistakes happen because I’m afraid of messing up.\n\nThere are definitely times where I’m trying hard to do something correctly and I still miss a detail, which is frustrating. So I guess I don’t know. The fear might make me more tense once I notice a problem, but I haven’t really noticed that being the main reason the lapses happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself disorganized, having trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines, beyond just forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that from the forgetting, honestly. Like, when you say disorganized, do you mean my space being messy, or more like not having a good system for what I’m supposed to do next?\n\nBecause I wouldn’t say I’m completely chaotic all the time, but I do feel like keeping track of things can get kind of… scattered. Sometimes I’ll have things in different places, like notes or reminders, and then I’m not sure which one I’m supposed to trust or what I already followed up on. But I don’t know if that’s disorganization exactly or just me trying to compensate and not always doing it well.\n\nAppointments and deadlines, I usually try to put things somewhere so I don’t lose track of them, but if there are a lot of moving pieces, it can feel like I’m holding it all in my head and then it gets messy. I guess I’ve noticed that I need more effort than I probably should to keep tasks straight. But I don’t know that I’d say it’s beyond forgetting as a totally separate thing. It’s kind of wrapped together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sustaining focus on tasks, even when you're interested in them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even if it’s something I care about getting right, my focus can get pulled off pretty easily.\n\nLike at work, if I’m trying to review call notes or look over numbers, I can be sitting there with every intention of finishing it, and then I’ll hear one conversation across the room—someone talking to a rep, or even just a few words that catch my attention—and before I realize it, I’m listening to that instead of what’s in front of me. Or sometimes it’s not even something around me. A random thought about something at home will pop in, like something I need to check or something I forgot to mention, and then I’ve drifted off the task without really deciding to.\n\nThat’s the frustrating part, because it’s not always that I’m bored or avoiding it. I might actually be interested in what I’m reviewing, or know it’s important, but my attention just kind of slides away. Then I have to reread the same note or go back over the same numbers because I’m not sure where I left off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to complete tasks because you lose interest or get distracted, rather than because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s closer to what happens. It’s not usually like I’m sitting there frozen because I’m worried I’ll do it wrong. It’s more that I start doing what I’m supposed to do, and then something else grabs my attention or feels urgent, and the original thing just kind of drops off.\n\nLike if my manager gives me three or four things to handle, I can start out really solid. I’ll be like, okay, I need to talk to this rep, send that follow-up email, update the coaching note, and check on whatever number or account issue. And I’ll usually begin with one of them right away. But then somebody walks up with a question, or there’s a call issue, or I notice something else that seems like it needs to be handled, and I get pulled into that.\n\nThen later—sometimes much later—I’ll realize I never sent the follow-up email, or I didn’t close out the coaching note, even though in my head it felt like I was “on it.” That’s the part that bothers me, because I didn’t mean to leave it unfinished. It’s not that I stopped because I didn’t care or because I was scared of making a mistake. It’s more like the task lost its place in line once something else interrupted me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes and forgetfulness would happen less often if you didn't worry about the potential consequences of making an error?\n\nPatient: I don’t think the worry is the main driver, honestly. I mean, I do worry after the fact, because there are consequences if I mess up a sales report or miss something I was supposed to check. But the mistake itself usually happens even when I’m trying to be careful.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep making these dumb mistakes that feel like they shouldn’t be happening. Like I’ll copy numbers from the team dashboard into a sales report, and later I’ll realize I grabbed the wrong number or put it in the wrong spot. Or there’ll be a procedure I’m supposed to review, and I’ll think I went through it, but I missed a line that mattered.\n\nAnd that’s what frustrates me, because in the moment I’m not thinking, “Oh no, what if I make an error?” and then messing up because I’m anxious. It’s more like I genuinely believe I checked it, or I thought I was paying attention, and then somehow something obvious slipped past me. So if I didn’t worry about consequences, I don’t know that it would happen less. I might feel less stressed about it, but I think the copying the wrong numbers or missing the line would still happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're supposed to be sitting still or focused on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice a lot, especially in meetings or anytime I’m supposed to just sit there and pay attention for a while.\n\nLike if we’re in a longer meeting at work, even if the topic matters and I know I need to listen, after a while it feels like my body is buzzing on the inside. I don’t know if that sounds weird, but it’s like I’m sitting still on the outside only because I’m forcing myself to. I’ll start shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my foot, messing with a pen, adjusting how I’m sitting over and over. Sometimes I’ll catch myself doing it and try to stop, but then I feel even more uncomfortable.\n\nAnd if the meeting goes on too long, I’ll start looking for a reason to get up, like grabbing water, going to the restroom, checking something at the printer—anything that makes it seem normal to move around for a minute. It’s not even always that I want to leave the meeting. It’s more like I need to reset my body because sitting still starts to feel almost physically irritating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not like I’m trying to be rude, but it happens more than I’d like, especially in meetings.\n\nSomeone will be explaining something, and I’ll think I already know where they’re going with it, so I jump in with an answer or a response before they’ve actually finished. In my head it feels like I’m being helpful or keeping things moving, but then they’ll finish their thought and I realize I answered the wrong point, or I misunderstood what they were actually asking. And then I’m sitting there like, okay, I just cut them off and also didn’t even answer the right thing.\n\nIt happens with my team too. Like if a rep starts telling me about an issue, I’ll sometimes jump to the solution before they’ve gotten through all the details. Then they’ll say, “No, that’s not the part I mean,” and I have to back up and let them finish. I do try to catch myself, but a lot of times the response is already coming out before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences of your actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so—at least in the way I talk in meetings. I don’t know if I’d describe it like I’m out doing reckless things all the time, but I definitely act before I’ve fully thought it through when someone else is talking.\n\nLike someone will be halfway through explaining something, and I’ll already have this answer forming in my head. I think I know where they’re going, so I just jump in. In the moment it feels almost automatic, like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose the thought or the conversation is moving too slowly. But then two seconds later I realize they weren’t even asking what I thought they were asking, or they had another detail coming that changes the whole point.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed because I’ve cut them off, and sometimes I’ve answered the wrong thing completely. It’s frustrating because I don’t mean to be disrespectful. I’m not trying to talk over people. It’s more like my mouth gets ahead of the part of me that should wait and listen to the rest of the sentence.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. I can be looking right at someone and still not really be following what they’re saying, which is embarrassing to admit.

At work, it happens a lot when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, or when someone is explaining something and there’s other stuff going on around me. Like one conversation across the room—just somebody talking about an account or laughing about something—can pull my attention away before I even realize it. And then suddenly I’m halfway listening to that instead of the person in front of me.

Or I’ll be trying to focus, and some random thought pops in my head about something at home, like whether I forgot to pay something or what we need from the store, and I’m gone for a minute. Then I come back and I’ve missed part of what they said, so I’m trying to piece it together from context without making it obvious.

It’s not that I don’t care what they’re saying. A lot of the time I really do care, especially if it’s work-related and I know I need the information. But my attention gets yanked away so easily, and by the time I notice, I’m already behind in the conversation.

Outside of work, I guess I can get tense if I’m dealing with something important or if I feel like I’m going to forget something or mess something up, but I haven’t really paid attention to it the same way.\n\nAt work it’s just more obvious because there’s a report, there’s a number, there’s a procedure, and then there’s proof that I got it wrong. Outside of work it’s harder to say. I might get frustrated or wound up about stuff, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as anxiety exactly. I’m not trying to dodge the question — I just don’t have a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself forgetting things outside of work, like appointments or tasks you need to do at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s not just work stuff. I wish it was, because then I could blame the reports or the pressure or whatever, but it happens at home too.\n\nLike I’ll forget to return calls I really need to make — even important ones, like calls from my attorney. I’ll see the missed call or the voicemail and think, “Okay, I need to call them back,” and then somehow the day keeps going and it just disappears from my head until later, or until there’s another message.\n\nBills are another one. I don’t mean I don’t know they exist. I’ll see the bill, or I’ll think about paying it, and then I’ll put it off like, “I’ll do that after dinner,” or “I’ll do it when I sit down,” and then next thing I know there’s a late notice and I’m mad at myself because it wasn’t like I didn’t have any warning.\n\nAnd with my kids, that one bothers me more emotionally. I’ll tell them I’ll pick something up or bring something home, and I mean it when I say it. Then I come home and they ask about it, and I realize I completely forgot. It makes me feel awful, because to them it probably feels like I didn’t care enough to remember, but in my head it just slipped away.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these memory lapses and mistakes?\n\nPatient: It’s been a real problem since I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago, something like that.\n\nI mean, I’m sure I forgot things before then like anybody does, but that’s when it started feeling like, “Okay, this is actually causing problems.” Once I had more reports, more procedures to keep track of, more things where if I missed a detail it showed up later, it became much more obvious. So I’d say around 14 months ago is when it really became noticeable and started affecting me in a bigger way.\n\nDoctor: Have these mistakes and memory lapses affected your relationships with your family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I don’t think it’s destroyed anything, but it definitely causes problems.\n\nAt home, the biggest thing is people feeling like they can’t count on me to follow through. Like if I say I’m going to do something, there’s this look sometimes, or a comment like, “Are you actually going to remember?” And I get why they say it, but it still stings. Because I’m not trying to blow people off or make extra work for anyone. I’ll genuinely mean it when I say I’m going to handle something, and then if I forget, it turns into an argument or this whole thing where I’m defending myself, but I also know I messed up.\n\nIt adds tension, especially with family. It makes me feel like I’m letting them down in these small repeated ways, and then those small things build up. People start reminding me more, or checking behind me, and then I get irritated because it feels like I’m being treated like a kid, but honestly I can’t fully blame them because I haven’t been reliable with some of it.\n\nAnd work-wise, it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review. So it’s not just me being hard on myself or thinking, “Oh, I made a couple mistakes.” It’s documented now, which was embarrassing and kind of scary. It made me realize this is affecting how people see me, not just how I feel inside.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you're feeling tense or on edge?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get tense, definitely, but I don’t know that I’ve paid close attention to whether my heart is racing or if I’m sweating or trembling.\n\nLike, if I’m stressed, I mostly notice it as being keyed up in my head, if that makes sense, or feeling wound up. But the physical stuff you’re asking about… I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed a clear pattern. Maybe it’s happened and I just didn’t clock it that way, but I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these mistakes and memory lapses are significantly interfering with your ability to function at work or enjoy your personal life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it’s interfering pretty significantly, especially at work. It’s not just little embarrassing moments anymore where I forget something and then fix it later. It’s gotten to the point that my manager actually put it in a formal review, which was… honestly, that hit me pretty hard. Once it’s written down like that, it feels less like “I’m having a rough stretch” and more like “this is affecting my job.”\n\nAnd at home, it’s not like I can’t enjoy anything ever, but it definitely takes some of the ease out of things. There’s conflict because people feel like they can’t count on me to follow through. I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then if I forget, it turns into frustration or reminders or people checking up on me. Then I feel bad, or I get defensive, and it kind of ruins the mood.\n\nSo, yeah… it’s affecting both. Work is where it feels most serious, because there are real consequences, but personally it’s wearing on me too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things in general, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I worry about things, but usually there’s some reason for it, at least in my mind. Like if something is actually pending or I know there’s a chance I forgot something, then yeah, I might keep thinking about it. But just worrying out of nowhere, for no clear reason… I’m not sure.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts as “no apparent reason,” because sometimes the reason is kind of vague, like just a sense that I’m missing something or that something’s going to come back on me. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly worrying about everything in general all the time. It’s more tied to specific situations, from what I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where these mistakes and memory lapses stopped happening for a while, or have they been consistent since they started?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say they’ve really stopped for any meaningful stretch, no. The point where it became a real problem was when I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago now, roughly.\n\nBefore that, I mean, I’m sure I had mistakes here and there like anybody, but it didn’t feel like this same ongoing issue. Since taking on that role, it’s been pretty consistent. There might be a better week or a day where I feel more on top of things, but I can’t think of a period where it just went away and I was like, “Okay, that’s over.” It’s more like it keeps showing up, and the setting I’m in now makes it harder to hide or recover from.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes and memory lapses are primarily caused by worrying about making mistakes or fear of not doing something correctly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do worry after I’ve made a mistake, or if I realize there’s something I might have missed, but I don’t know that the worry is what causes it in the first place.\n\nIt feels more like… I’ll be trying to keep track of several things, and then something slips, and then the worry kicks in afterward because now I’m thinking, “Did I forget something else?” or “Is this going to come back on me?” But I wouldn’t confidently say the mistakes happen because I’m afraid of messing up.\n\nThere are definitely times where I’m trying hard to do something correctly and I still miss a detail, which is frustrating. So I guess I don’t know. The fear might make me more tense once I notice a problem, but I haven’t really noticed that being the main reason the lapses happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself disorganized, having trouble keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines, beyond just forgetting things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that from the forgetting, honestly. Like, when you say disorganized, do you mean my space being messy, or more like not having a good system for what I’m supposed to do next?\n\nBecause I wouldn’t say I’m completely chaotic all the time, but I do feel like keeping track of things can get kind of… scattered. Sometimes I’ll have things in different places, like notes or reminders, and then I’m not sure which one I’m supposed to trust or what I already followed up on. But I don’t know if that’s disorganization exactly or just me trying to compensate and not always doing it well.\n\nAppointments and deadlines, I usually try to put things somewhere so I don’t lose track of them, but if there are a lot of moving pieces, it can feel like I’m holding it all in my head and then it gets messy. I guess I’ve noticed that I need more effort than I probably should to keep tasks straight. But I don’t know that I’d say it’s beyond forgetting as a totally separate thing. It’s kind of wrapped together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sustaining focus on tasks, even when you're interested in them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even if it’s something I care about getting right, my focus can get pulled off pretty easily.\n\nLike at work, if I’m trying to review call notes or look over numbers, I can be sitting there with every intention of finishing it, and then I’ll hear one conversation across the room—someone talking to a rep, or even just a few words that catch my attention—and before I realize it, I’m listening to that instead of what’s in front of me. Or sometimes it’s not even something around me. A random thought about something at home will pop in, like something I need to check or something I forgot to mention, and then I’ve drifted off the task without really deciding to.\n\nThat’s the frustrating part, because it’s not always that I’m bored or avoiding it. I might actually be interested in what I’m reviewing, or know it’s important, but my attention just kind of slides away. Then I have to reread the same note or go back over the same numbers because I’m not sure where I left off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to complete tasks because you lose interest or get distracted, rather than because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s closer to what happens. It’s not usually like I’m sitting there frozen because I’m worried I’ll do it wrong. It’s more that I start doing what I’m supposed to do, and then something else grabs my attention or feels urgent, and the original thing just kind of drops off.\n\nLike if my manager gives me three or four things to handle, I can start out really solid. I’ll be like, okay, I need to talk to this rep, send that follow-up email, update the coaching note, and check on whatever number or account issue. And I’ll usually begin with one of them right away. But then somebody walks up with a question, or there’s a call issue, or I notice something else that seems like it needs to be handled, and I get pulled into that.\n\nThen later—sometimes much later—I’ll realize I never sent the follow-up email, or I didn’t close out the coaching note, even though in my head it felt like I was “on it.” That’s the part that bothers me, because I didn’t mean to leave it unfinished. It’s not that I stopped because I didn’t care or because I was scared of making a mistake. It’s more like the task lost its place in line once something else interrupted me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes and forgetfulness would happen less often if you didn't worry about the potential consequences of making an error?\n\nPatient: I don’t think the worry is the main driver, honestly. I mean, I do worry after the fact, because there are consequences if I mess up a sales report or miss something I was supposed to check. But the mistake itself usually happens even when I’m trying to be careful.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I keep making these dumb mistakes that feel like they shouldn’t be happening. Like I’ll copy numbers from the team dashboard into a sales report, and later I’ll realize I grabbed the wrong number or put it in the wrong spot. Or there’ll be a procedure I’m supposed to review, and I’ll think I went through it, but I missed a line that mattered.\n\nAnd that’s what frustrates me, because in the moment I’m not thinking, “Oh no, what if I make an error?” and then messing up because I’m anxious. It’s more like I genuinely believe I checked it, or I thought I was paying attention, and then somehow something obvious slipped past me. So if I didn’t worry about consequences, I don’t know that it would happen less. I might feel less stressed about it, but I think the copying the wrong numbers or missing the line would still happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're supposed to be sitting still or focused on a task?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things I notice a lot, especially in meetings or anytime I’m supposed to just sit there and pay attention for a while.\n\nLike if we’re in a longer meeting at work, even if the topic matters and I know I need to listen, after a while it feels like my body is buzzing on the inside. I don’t know if that sounds weird, but it’s like I’m sitting still on the outside only because I’m forcing myself to. I’ll start shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my foot, messing with a pen, adjusting how I’m sitting over and over. Sometimes I’ll catch myself doing it and try to stop, but then I feel even more uncomfortable.\n\nAnd if the meeting goes on too long, I’ll start looking for a reason to get up, like grabbing water, going to the restroom, checking something at the printer—anything that makes it seem normal to move around for a minute. It’s not even always that I want to leave the meeting. It’s more like I need to reset my body because sitting still starts to feel almost physically irritating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not like I’m trying to be rude, but it happens more than I’d like, especially in meetings.\n\nSomeone will be explaining something, and I’ll think I already know where they’re going with it, so I jump in with an answer or a response before they’ve actually finished. In my head it feels like I’m being helpful or keeping things moving, but then they’ll finish their thought and I realize I answered the wrong point, or I misunderstood what they were actually asking. And then I’m sitting there like, okay, I just cut them off and also didn’t even answer the right thing.\n\nIt happens with my team too. Like if a rep starts telling me about an issue, I’ll sometimes jump to the solution before they’ve gotten through all the details. Then they’ll say, “No, that’s not the part I mean,” and I have to back up and let them finish. I do try to catch myself, but a lot of times the response is already coming out before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking through the consequences of your actions?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so—at least in the way I talk in meetings. I don’t know if I’d describe it like I’m out doing reckless things all the time, but I definitely act before I’ve fully thought it through when someone else is talking.\n\nLike someone will be halfway through explaining something, and I’ll already have this answer forming in my head. I think I know where they’re going, so I just jump in. In the moment it feels almost automatic, like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose the thought or the conversation is moving too slowly. But then two seconds later I realize they weren’t even asking what I thought they were asking, or they had another detail coming that changes the whole point.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed because I’ve cut them off, and sometimes I’ve answered the wrong thing completely. It’s frustrating because I don’t mean to be disrespectful. I’m not trying to talk over people. It’s more like my mouth gets ahead of the part of me that should wait and listen to the rest of the sentence.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to listen to others when they are speaking, often finding your mind wandering or having trouble following the conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I can be looking right at someone and still not really be following what they’re saying, which is embarrassing to admit.\n\nAt work, it happens a lot when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, or when someone is explaining something and there’s other stuff going on around me. Like one conversation across the room—just somebody talking about an account or laughing about something—can pull my attention away before I even realize it. And then suddenly I’m halfway listening to that instead of the person in front of me.\n\nOr I’ll be trying to focus, and some random thought pops in my head about something at home, like whether I forgot to pay something or what we need from the store, and I’m gone for a minute. Then I come back and I’ve missed part of what they said, so I’m trying to piece it together from context without making it obvious.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care what they’re saying. A lot of the time I really do care, especially if it’s work-related and I know I need the information. But my attention gets yanked away so easily, and by the time I notice, I’m already behind in the conversation."
